**John Robert Bolton** (born November 20, 1948) is an American attorney, diplomat, [Republican](32070.md) consultant, and political commentator. He served as the 25th [United States ambassador to the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_ambassador_to_the_United_Nations) from 2005 to 2006, and as the 26th [United States national security advisor](44028.md) from 2018 to 2019.

Bolton also acted as a [United States assistant attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Assistant_Attorney_General) for President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) from 1985 to 1989. He served in the [State Department](31975.md) as the [assistant secretary of state for international organization affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assistant_Secretary_of_State_for_International_Organization_Affairs) from 1989 to 1993, and the [under secretary of state for arms control and international security affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under_Secretary_of_State_for_Arms_Control_and_International_Security) from 2001 to 2005. He was an advocate of the [Iraq War](5043324.md) as a Director of the [Project for the New American Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_for_the_New_American_Century), which favored going to war with Iraq.[^cite_note-politico1-8]

He was the U.S. Ambassador to the United Nations from August 2005 to December 2006, as a [recess appointee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recess_appointment) by President [George W. Bush](3414021.md).[^cite_note-NYMagSenior-9] He stepped down at the end of his recess appointment in December 2006[^cite_note-boltonresigns-10][^cite_note-bushaccept-11] because he was unlikely to win confirmation in the [Senate](24909346.md), of which the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) had control at the time.[^cite_note-boltonresigns-10][^cite_note-12] Bolton later served as National Security Advisor to President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) from April 2018 to September 2019. He repeatedly called for the termination of the [Iran nuclear deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_deal), from which the [U.S. withdrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_withdrawal_from_the_Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action) in May 2018. He wrote a best-selling book about his tenure in the [Trump administration](52231341.md), *[The Room Where It Happened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Room_Where_It_Happened)*, published in 2020.[^cite_note-Timesbolton-13]

Bolton is widely considered a foreign policy [hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_hawk) and advocates military action and [regime change by the U.S.](37258993.md) in [Iran](14653.md), [Syria](7515849.md), [Libya](17633.md), [Venezuela](32374.md), [Cuba](5042481.md), [Yemen](350939.md), and [North Korea](21255.md).[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-politico1-8][^cite_note-16] A member of the Republican Party, his political views have been described as [American nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_nationalist),[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] and [neoconservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoconservative),[^cite_note-\:1-23] although Bolton rejects the last term.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26] He is a former senior fellow at the [American Enterprise Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Enterprise_Institute) (AEI)[^cite_note-27] and a [Fox News Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Channel) commentator. He was a foreign policy adviser to 2012 Republican presidential nominee [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney).[^cite_note-limp-wristed-28] In October 2025, Bolton [was indicted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecution_of_John_Bolton) on eight counts of unlawful transmission and ten counts of unlawful retention of national defense information between 2018 and 2025.[^cite_note-29] On June 26, 2026, Bolton pled guilty.[^cite_note-30]

In 2022, Iranian [IRGC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IRGC) member Shahram Poursafi was charged with attempting to hire individuals in the United States to assassinate Bolton.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32]

## Early life



Bolton in [McDonogh School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonogh_School)'s 1966 yearbook

Bolton was born on November 20, 1948, in [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore), Maryland, the son of Virginia Clara "Ginny" (née Godfrey), a housewife, and Edward Jackson "Jack" Bolton, a Baltimore fireman.[^cite_note-\:10-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 4 </sup> He grew up in the working-class neighborhood of [Yale Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Heights_\(Baltimore\)), and won a scholarship to the [McDonogh School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonogh_School) in [Owings Mills, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owings_Mills,_Maryland), graduating in 1966.[^cite_note-\:10-33] He also ran the school's Students For [Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater) campaign in 1964.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37]

Bolton attended [Yale College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_College), earning a [Bachelor of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts) and graduating *[summa cum laude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summa_cum_laude)* in 1970. He was a member of the [Yale Political Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Political_Union). He attended [Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School) from 1971 to 1974, where he shared classes and student housing with his friend [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas), earning a [Juris Doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juris_Doctor) in 1974.[^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 12 </sup>

In 1972, Bolton was a summer intern for Vice President [Spiro Agnew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiro_Agnew).[^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 12–13 </sup>[^cite_note-intern-38] He was hired for the position by [David Keene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Keene).[^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 12–13 </sup>

### Vietnam War

Bolton was a supporter of the [Vietnam War](32611.md),[^cite_note-uruqhart-39] but avoided combat through a student deferment followed by enlistment in the [Maryland National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland_National_Guard).[^cite_note-\:10-33][^cite_note-\:2-40][^cite_note-41] During the 1969 [Vietnam War draft lottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draft_lottery_\(1969\)), Bolton drew number 185. (Draft numbers were randomly assigned to birth dates; someone born on December 31 could have received Number 1. Numbers 1 to 195 were eventually called up.)[^cite_note-42] As a result of the Johnson and Nixon administrations' decisions to rely largely on the draft rather than on the reserve forces, joining a Guard or Reserve unit became a way to reduce the chances of service in Vietnam.[^cite_note-43]

Before graduating from Yale College in 1970, Bolton enlisted in the [Maryland Army National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland_Army_National_Guard) rather than waiting to find out if his draft number would be called.[^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 11 </sup>[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45] He attended Active Duty for Training (ADT) at [Fort Polk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Polk), Louisiana, from July to November 1970.[^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 11 </sup> After serving in the National Guard for four years, he served in the [United States Army Reserve](1187028.md) until the end of his enlistment two years later.[^cite_note-UNamb-7]

He wrote in his Yale 25th reunion book: "I confess I had no desire to die in a Southeast Asian rice paddy. I considered the war in Vietnam already lost."[^cite_note-\:2-40] In a 2007 interview, Bolton explained his comment in the reunion book saying his decision to avoid service in Vietnam was because "by the time I was about to graduate in 1970, it was clear to me that opponents of the Vietnam War had made it certain we could not prevail, and that I had no great interest in going there to have [Teddy Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy) give it back to the people I might die to take it away from."[^cite_note-uruqhart-39][^cite_note-46]

In his 2007 book, *Surrender Is Not an Option*, Bolton described his perception of the war as a "futile struggle", and that "dying for your country was one thing, but dying to gain territory that antiwar forces in Congress would simply return to the enemy seemed ludicrous to me. Looking back, I am not terribly proud of this calculation..."[^cite_note-surrender-35]<sup>: 11 </sup>

## Career

### Attorney

From 1974 to 1981, Bolton was an associate at the [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) office of [Covington & Burling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covington_&_Burling); he returned to the firm again from 1983 to 1985. Bolton was also a partner in the law firm of Lerner, Reed, Bolton & McManus, from 1993 to 1999.[^cite_note-bio_44.html-47][^cite_note-2976.htm-48][^cite_note-\:3-49] He was [of counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Of_counsel) in the Washington, D.C. office of [Kirkland & Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkland_&_Ellis) from 2008 until his appointment as National Security Advisor in 2018.[^cite_note-auto-50][^cite_note-McL-51][^cite_note-wadhams-52] In September 2015, Freedom Capital Investment Management appointed Bolton as a senior advisor.[^cite_note-53]

### Reagan and George H.W. Bush administrations

During the [Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan) and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_H._W._Bush) administrations, his governmental roles were within the [State Department](31975.md), the [Justice Department](52563.md), and the [U.S. Agency for International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development). He was a [protégé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prot%C3%A9g%C3%A9) of conservative [North Carolina](21650.md) Senator [Jesse Helms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Helms).[^cite_note-54]

His Justice Department position as an assistant attorney general required him to advance Reagan administration positions, including[^cite_note-Vest-55] opposition to financial [reparations](29967971.md) to [Japanese-Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese-American) held in World War II-era [internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_American_internment);[^cite_note-56] the insistence on Reagan's [executive privilege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_privilege) during [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist)'s chief justice confirmation hearings, when Congress asked for memos written by Rehnquist as a [Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_M._Nixon) Justice Department official;[^cite_note-57] shepherding the judicial nomination process for [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia);[^cite_note-Vest-55] and the framing of a bill to control [illegal immigration](5044573.md) as an essential [drug war](1181646.md) measure.[^cite_note-Vest-55] He was also involved in the [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair).[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60]

Bolton's government service included positions such as:

- Assistant secretary for [International Organization Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_International_Organization_Affairs) at the Department of State (1989–1993)

- [Assistant Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Assistant_Attorney_General) for the [Civil Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice_Civil_Division) (1988–1989)

  [^cite_note-61]

- [Assistant Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Assistant_Attorney_General) for the [Office of Legislative Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Justice_Office_of_Legislative_Affairs), Department of Justice (1985–1988)

  [^cite_note-UNamb-7]

- Assistant administrator for program and policy coordination, USAID (1982–1983); and

- General counsel, USAID (1981–1982).

  [^cite_note-bio_44.html-47]

  [^cite_note-2976.htm-48]

  &#x20;While working for USAID, Lynne Finney, a legal adviser for the agency, alleged that Bolton threatened to fire her for refusing to lobby for the deregulation of baby formula in developing nations

  [^cite_note-62]

- Commissioner, [United States Commission on International Religious Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Commission_on_International_Religious_Freedom) (1999-2001)

  [^cite_note-63]

## Under Secretary of State (2001–2005)



Bolton joins Secretary of Defense [Donald H. Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_H._Rumsfeld) in negotiations with Rumsfeld's Russian counterpart in 2001.

Bolton worked as the Under Secretary of State for Arms Control and International Security, sworn into this position on May 11, 2001. In this role, a key area of his responsibility was the prevention of proliferation of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction).

Bolton negotiated so-called "Article 98" agreements with countries to prohibit them from turning Americans over to the [International Criminal Court](14880.md), which is not recognized by the U.S.[^cite_note-\:11-64][^cite_note-\:13-65] Bolton said the decision to pull out of the ICC was the "happiest moment" of his political career to date.[^cite_note-\:11-64][^cite_note-economisticc-66]

Bolton has often been accused of attempting to pressure the intelligence community to endorse his views.[^cite_note-\:5-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-\:7-69] According to former coworkers, Bolton withheld information that ran counter to his goals from Secretary of State [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) on multiple occasions, and from Powell's successor [Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice) on at least one occasion.[^cite_note-\:7-69][^cite_note-70]

### Weapons of mass destruction

Bolton was instrumental in derailing a 2001 [biological weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_weapon) conference in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva) convened to endorse a UN proposal to enforce the 1972 [Biological Weapons Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_Weapons_Convention). He argued that the plan would have jeopardized U.S. national security by allowing spot inspections of suspected U.S. weapons sites.[^cite_note-71]

In May 2002, Bolton gave a speech entitled "[Beyond the Axis of Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil#Beyond_the_Axis_of_Evil)" in response to President Bush's [State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_State_of_the_Union_Address) (where Bush characterized Iran, Iraq, and North Korea as part of an "Axis of Evil"). Bolton added three more nations to be grouped with the aforementioned [rogue states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogue_state): Cuba, [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi#Great_Socialist_People's_Libyan_Arab_Jamahiriya_\(1977%E2%80%932011\)), and Syria. Bolton said they were all "[state sponsors of terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_sponsors_of_terrorism) that are pursuing or who have the potential to pursue [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction) (WMD) or have the capability to do so in violation of their treaty obligations."[^cite_note-bolton-beyond-72] During his time as Under Secretary of State, Bolton "sought to block, and often succeeded in sabotaging" the negotiations that Secretary of State [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell) had conducted with North Korea.[^cite_note-73]

Also in 2002, Bolton is said to have flown to Europe to demand the resignation of Brazilian [José Bustani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Bustani), head of the [Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons](36832.md) (OPCW), and to have orchestrated his removal at a special session of the organization.[^cite_note-74] Bustani was deemed to be an obstacle in creating the case for the invasion of Iraq.[^cite_note-75] The United Nations' highest administrative tribunal later condemned the action as an "unacceptable violation" of principles protecting international civil servants. Bustani had been unanimously re-elected for a four-year term—with strong U.S. support—in May 2000, and in 2001 was praised for his leadership by [Colin Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colin_Powell).[^cite_note-76] According to Bustani, John Bolton demanded that he step down in 24 hours, adding, "We know where your children are."[^cite_note-77]

He also pushed for reduced funding for the [Nunn–Lugar Cooperative Threat Reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunn%E2%80%93Lugar_Cooperative_Threat_Reduction) program to halt the [proliferation](22158.md) of [nuclear materials](2181563.md).[^cite_note-ivins-78] At the same time, he was involved in the implementation of the [Proliferation Security Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proliferation_Security_Initiative), working with a number of countries to intercept the trafficking in [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction) and in materials for use in building nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-79]

Democratic Congressman [Henry Waxman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Waxman) alleged that Bolton played a role in encouraging the inclusion of the statement that [British Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Intelligence) had determined Iraq [attempted to procure yellowcake uranium from Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_uranium_forgeries) in Bush's [2003 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_State_of_the_Union_Address).[^cite_note-20050301112122-90349.pdf-80] These statements were claimed by critics of the President to be partly based on documents found to be forged.[^cite_note-81] Waxman's allegations could not be confirmed, as they were based on classified documents.[^cite_note-20050301112122-90349.pdf-80] Bolton stated in June 2004 congressional testimony that Iran was lying about [enriched uranium](37555.md) contamination: "Another unmistakable indicator of Iran's intentions is the pattern of repeatedly lying to ... the IAEA ... when evidence of uranium enriched to 36 percent was found, it attributed this to contamination from imported centrifuge parts." However, later [isotope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotope) analysis supported Iran's explanation of foreign contamination for most of the observed enriched uranium.[^cite_note-82] At their August 2005 meeting the IAEA's Board of Governors concluded: "Based on the information currently available to the Agency, the results of that analysis tend, on balance, to support Iran's statement about the foreign origin of most of the observed HEU \[highly enriched uranium] contamination."[^cite_note-83]

### Diplomacy

According to an article in *[The New Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Republic)*, Bolton was highly successful in pushing his agenda, but his bluntness made him many enemies. "Iran's Foreign Ministry has called Bolton 'rude' and 'undiplomatic'."[^cite_note-84] In response to critics, Bolton says his record "demonstrates clear support for effective multilateral diplomacy." Bush administration officials have stated that his past statements would allow him to negotiate from a powerful position. "It's like the Palestinians' having to negotiate with \[Israeli Prime Minister] Ariel Sharon. If you have a deal, you know you have a deal," an anonymous official told [CNN](62028.md).[^cite_note-Bush_nominates_Bolton-85] He also "won widespread praise for his work establishing the Proliferation Security Initiative, a voluntary agreement supported (at the time) by 60 countries".[^cite_note-86]

### Interventions

In July 2022, during an interview with CNN reporter [Jake Tapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Tapper), denying that President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s involvement in the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md) amounted to an attempt to overthrow the U.S. government, Bolton admitted to his personal involvement in planning unspecified [*coups d'état*](25914154.md) in foreign countries.[^cite_note-87]

> "As somebody who has helped plan coups d'etat — not here but, you know, other places — it takes a lot of work, and that's not what \[President Donald Trump] did"

— John Bolton[^cite_note-88]

According to [Reuters](18998750.md), "it is highly unusual for U.S. officials to openly acknowledge their role in stoking unrest in foreign countries."[^cite_note-89]

### Unsubstantiated claims about a Cuban WMD program

In 2002, Bolton delivered a speech at the Heritage Foundation where he accused Cuba of having a secret biological weapons program, and of collaborating with Libya and Iran.[^cite_note-\:3-49][^cite_note-\:4-90][^cite_note-\:6-91] Bolton asserted, "The United States believes that Cuba has at least a limited offensive biological warfare research and development effort. Cuba has provided dual-use biotechnology to other rogue states."[^cite_note-\:6-91][^cite_note-92] Bolton made the remarks a week before former President Jimmy Carter was scheduled to meet Fidel Castro in Cuba, becoming the first U.S. president since the Cuban Revolution to visit Cuba in an effort to build bridges between the two countries.[^cite_note-\:6-91]

The State Department's chief bioweapons analyst refused to approve the accusation made in the speech, telling Bolton that the State Department did not have evidence to substantiate Bolton's accusation.[^cite_note-\:3-49][^cite_note-\:4-90][^cite_note-\:6-91][^cite_note-\:5-67] Subsequently, Bolton berated the analyst, unsuccessfully sought to fire him, began to exclude the analyst's supervisor from meetings, and tried to transfer the analyst to a different office.[^cite_note-\:3-49][^cite_note-\:4-90][^cite_note-\:6-91] Bolton was also alleged to have sought to punish other intelligence officers who refused to endorse his claims about Cuba.[^cite_note-ivins-78][^cite_note-\:6-91][^cite_note-BOLTON-04-11-05-93]

[Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University) international relations scholar Richard K. Betts described the reports about Bolton's pressure as "most blatant top-down pressure on intelligence" in the Bush administration.[^cite_note-94] Bolton claims the issue was procedural rather than related to the content of his speech and that the officers, who did not work under him, behaved unprofessionally.[^cite_note-\:4-90]

In April 2004, Bolton again accused Cuba of being a "terrorist and (biological weapons) threat to the United States."[^cite_note-95] Experts at the time disputed the veracity of Bolton's claims, saying the evidence in support of the claim was weak.[^cite_note-96] In September 2004 and in the wake of the failure to locate WMDs in Iraq, the Bush administration backed off claims that Cuba had an active biological weapons program.[^cite_note-97]

## Ambassador to the United Nations (2005–2006)



President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) announces Bolton's nomination as U.S. ambassador to the UN. Secretary of State [Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice) looks on.

On March 7, 2005, Bolton was nominated to the post of [United States Ambassador to the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Nations) by President [George W. Bush](3414021.md).[^cite_note-Bush_nominates_Bolton-85][^cite_note-98] Bolton said previously at a forum that "there is no such thing as the United Nations", and "\[i]f the U.N. secretary building in New York lost 10 stories, it wouldn't make a bit of difference".[^cite_note-Bush_nominates_Bolton-85] Several officials from U.N. Security Council member states were shocked that Bush would nominate someone they believed had a known antipathy toward the United Nations.[^cite_note-Bush_nominates_Bolton-85] As a result of a Democratic [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster), he was recess-appointed to the post on August 1, 2005. Bolton's nomination received strong support from [Republicans](32070.md) but faced heavy opposition from Democrats due initially to concerns about his strongly expressed views on the United Nations.[^cite_note-99]

Holding a 10–8 majority in the Senate Foreign Relations Committee (tasked with vetting ambassadorial nominees), the Republican leadership hoped to send Bolton's nomination to the full Senate with a positive recommendation. Concern among some Republicans on the committee, however, prompted the leadership to avoid losing such a motion and instead to send the nomination forward with no recommendation. In the full Senate, Republican support for the nomination remained uncertain, with the most vocal Republican critic, [Ohio](22199.md) Senator [George V. Voinovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_V._Voinovich), circulating a letter urging his Republican colleagues to oppose the nomination.[^cite_note-100]

Democrats insisted that a vote on the nomination was premature, given the resistance of the White House to share classified documents related to Bolton's alleged actions. The Republican leadership moved on two occasions to end debate, but because a supermajority of 60 votes is needed to end debate, the leadership was unable to muster the required votes with only a 55–44 majority in the body. An earlier agreement between moderates in both parties to prevent filibustering of nominees was interpreted by the Democrats to relate only to judicial nominees,[^cite_note-101] not ambassadorships, although the leader of the effort, Sen. John McCain, said the spirit of the agreement was to include all nominees.

On November 9, 2006, Bush, only days after losing both houses to a Democratic majority, sent the nomination[^cite_note-102] for Bolton to continue as representative for the United States at the UN.[^cite_note-103] He said: "I believe that the leaders of both political parties must try to work through our differences. And I believe we will be able to work through differences. I reassured the House and Senate leaders that I intend to work with the new Congress in a bipartisan way to address issues confronting this country."[^cite_note-104]

### 2005 nomination, Senate confirmation hearings

On April 11, 2005, the [Senate Foreign Relations Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Foreign_Relations_Committee) reviewed Bolton's qualifications. Bolton said he and his colleagues "view the U.N. as an important component of our diplomacy" and will work to solve its problems and enhance its strengths,[^cite_note-105] echoing Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice's words from a month earlier.[^cite_note-106]

On the first day of the hearings, Republican committee chairman [Richard Lugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Lugar) criticized Bolton for ignoring the "policy consequences" of his statements, saying diplomatic speech "should never be undertaken simply to score international debating points to appeal to segments of the U.S. public opinion or to validate a personal point of view."[^cite_note-107] The committee's top Democrat, [Joe Biden](145422.md), compared sending Bolton to the UN to sending a "bull into a china shop," and expressed "grave concern" about Bolton's "diplomatic temperament" and his record: "In my judgment, your judgment about how to deal with the emerging threats have not been particularly useful," Biden said.[^cite_note-108] Republican Senator [George Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Allen_\(U.S._politician\)) said Bolton had the "experience," "knowledge," "background," "and the right principles to come into the United Nations at this time," calling him "the absolute perfect person for the job."[^cite_note-109] Democratic Senator [Russ Feingold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russ_Feingold) asked Bolton about what he would have done had the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md) occurred while he was ambassador to the United Nations, and criticized his answer—which focused on logistics—as "amazingly passive."[^cite_note-110]

On the second day, April 12, 2005, the Senate panel focused on allegations discussed above that Bolton pressured intelligence analysts. Calling Bolton a "serial abuser", former State Department intelligence chief [Carl W. Ford Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_W._Ford_Jr.) said, "I've never seen anybody quite like Secretary Bolton ... I don't have a second, third or fourth in terms of the way that he abuses his power and authority with little people." Ford contradicted Bolton's earlier testimony, saying: "I had been asked for the first time to fire an intelligence analyst for what he had said and done." Ford also characterized Bolton as a "[kiss-up, kick-down](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiss_up_kick_down) sort of guy", implying that he was always ready to please whoever had authority over him, while having very little regard for people working under him.[^cite_note-111]

Lugar, who criticized Bolton at his April 11 hearing, said the "paramount issue" was supporting Bush's nominee. He conceded that "bluntness ... may be required", even though it is not "very good diplomacy".[^cite_note-112] [Lincoln Chafee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Chafee), the key member for Bolton's approval, said "the bar is very high" for rejecting the president's nominees, suggesting that Bolton would make it to the Senate.[^cite_note-113]

On April 19, Democrats, with support from Voinovich, forced Lugar to delay the committee vote on Bolton's nomination until May. The debate concerning his nomination raged in the Senate prior to the [Memorial Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Day) recess. Two other Republicans on the Foreign Relations Committee, Chafee and [Chuck Hagel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Hagel), also expressed serious concerns about the Bolton nomination.[^cite_note-NYT-114] Asked on April 20 if he was now less inclined to support the nomination, Chafee said, "That would be accurate." He elaborated that Bolton's prospects were "hard to predict" but said he expected that "the administration is really going to put some pressure on Senator Voinovich. Then it comes to the rest of us \[who] have had some reservations."[^cite_note-NYT-114]

On April 20, it emerged that Melody Townsel, a former USAID contractor, had reported to the Senate Foreign Relations Committee that Bolton had used inflammatory language and thrown objects in the course of her work activities in Moscow. Townsel's encounter with Bolton occurred when she served as a whistleblower against a poorly performing minority contractor for USAID, IBTCI. Townsel told the Senate Foreign Relations Committee staff that Bolton had made derogatory remarks about her [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) and weight, among other workplace improprieties. In an official interview with Senate Foreign Relation Committee staff, Townsel detailed her accusations against Bolton, which were confirmed by Canadian designer Uno Ramat, who had served as an IBTCI employee and one of Townsel's AID colleagues. *Time* magazine, among other publications, verified Townsel's accusations and Ramat's supporting testimony, and Townsel's story was transcribed and entered into the official Senate committee record. Townsel, who was an employee of Young & Rubicam at the time of her encounter with Bolton, continued working for the company on a variety of other USAID projects.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117]

On April 22, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* and other media reported that Bolton's former boss, Colin Powell, personally opposed the nomination and had been in personal contact with Chafee and Hagel. The same day, [Reuters](18998750.md) reported that a spokesman for Senator [Lisa Murkowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Murkowski) (R-Alaska) had said the Senator felt the committee "did the right thing delaying the vote on Bolton in light of the recent information presented to the committee."[^cite_note-118]

Also on May 11, *[Newsweek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsweek)* reported allegations that the American position at the 7th Review Conference in May 2005 of the [Nuclear Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Proliferation_Treaty) had been undercut by Bolton's "absence without leave" during the nomination fight, quoting anonymous sources "close to the negotiations".[^cite_note-119]

### Democrats' filibuster

On May 26, 2005, Senate Democrats postponed the vote on Bolton's UN nomination. The Republican leadership failed to gain enough support to pass a cloture motion on the floor debate over Bolton, and minority leader [Harry Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Reid) conceded the move signaled the "first [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster) of the year." The Democrats claimed that key documents regarding Bolton and his career at the Department of State were being withheld by the Bush administration. [Scott McClellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_McClellan), White House press secretary, responded by saying, "Just 72 hours after all the good will and bipartisanship (over a deal on judicial nominees), it's disappointing to see the Democratic leadership resort back to such a partisan approach."[^cite_note-120]

The failure of the Senate to end debate on Bolton's nomination provided one surprise for some: Senate Majority Leader [Bill Frist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Frist) (R-Tennessee) voted against [cloture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloture) for procedural reasons, so he could bring up a cloture vote in the future.[^cite_note-121] (Although Voinovich once spoke against confirming Bolton, he voted for cloture.) Senator [John Thune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Thune) (R-South Dakota) voted to end debate but announced that he would vote against Bolton in the up-or-down vote as a protest against the government's plans to close a military base ([Ellsworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellsworth_Air_Force_Base)) in his home state.

On June 20, 2005, the Senate voted again on cloture. The vote failed 54–38, six votes short of ending debate. That marked an increase of two "no" votes, including the defection of Voinovich, who switched his previous "yes" vote and urged President Bush to pick another nominee (Democrats [Mark Pryor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Pryor), [Mary Landrieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Landrieu) and [Ben Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Nelson) voted to end debate both times). On June 21, Frist expressed his view that attempting another vote would be pointless, but later that day, following a lunch at the White House, changed his position, saying he would continue to push for an up-or-down vote.[^cite_note-122] Voinovich later recanted his opposition and stated that if Bolton were renominated he would have supported the nomination.[^cite_note-123]

### Accusations of false statement

On July 28, 2005, it was revealed that a statement made by Bolton on forms submitted to the Senate was false. Bolton indicated that in the prior five years he had not been questioned in any investigation, but in fact he had been interviewed by the State Department's Inspector General as part of an investigation into the sources of pre-war claims of weapons of mass destruction in Iraq. After insisting for weeks that Bolton had testified truthfully on the form, the State Department reversed itself, saying Bolton had simply forgotten about the investigation.[^cite_note-124]

### Recess appointment

On August 1, 2005, Bush officially made a [recess appointment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recess_appointment) of Bolton, installing him as Permanent U.S. Representative to the UN. A recess appointment lasts until the next session of Congress ends or until the individual is renominated and confirmed by the Senate. During the announcement, Bush said, "This post is too important to leave vacant any longer, especially during a war and a vital debate about U.N. reform."[^cite_note-125] Democrats criticized the appointment, and Senator Christopher Dodd (D-CT) of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee said Bolton would lack credibility in the U.N. because he lacked Senate confirmation.[^cite_note-126] U.N. Secretary-General [Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan) welcomed Mr. Bolton, but told reporters that the new ambassador should consult with others as the administration continued to press for changes at the United Nations.[^cite_note-127]

### Term at the United Nations

*[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* called Bolton "the most controversial ambassador ever sent by America to the United Nations." Some colleagues in the UN appreciated the goals Bolton was trying to achieve, but not his abrasive style.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, in its editorial *The Shame of the United Nations*, praised Bolton's stance on "reforming the disgraceful [United Nations Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Commission)",[^cite_note-130] saying "John Bolton, is right; Secretary-General Kofi Annan is wrong." The *Times* also said the commission at that time was composed of "some of the world's most abusive regimes" who used their membership as cover to continue their abusiveness.

Bolton also opposed the proposed replacement for the Human Rights Commission, the [UN Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Council), as not going far enough for reform, saying: "We want a butterfly. We don't intend to put lipstick on a caterpillar and call it a success."[^cite_note-131]

### 2006 nomination

Bush announced his intention to renominate Bolton for confirmation as U.N. ambassador at the beginning of 2006, and a new confirmation hearing was held on July 27, 2006, in the hope of completing the process before the expiration of Bolton's recess appointment at the end of the 109th Congress.[^cite_note-D855-132] Voinovich, who had previously stood in opposition to Bolton, had amended his views and determined that Bolton was doing a "good job" as UN ambassador; in February 2006, he said "I spend a lot of time with John on the phone. I think he is really working very constructively to move forward."[^cite_note-133]

Over the summer and during the fall election campaign, no action was taken on the nomination because Chafee, who was in a difficult re-election campaign, blocked a [Senate Foreign Relations Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Foreign_Relations) vote.[^cite_note-134] Without his concurrence, the SFRC would have been deadlocked 9–9, and the nomination could not have gone to the Senate floor for a full vote. Bush formally resubmitted the nomination on November 9, 2006, immediately following a midterm election that would give control of the [110th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/110th_United_States_Congress) to the Democratic party.[^cite_note-135] Chafee, who had just lost his re-election bid, issued a statement saying he would vote against recommending Bolton for a Senate vote, citing what he considered to be a mandate from the recent election results: "On Tuesday, the American people sent a clear message of dissatisfaction with the foreign policy approach of the Bush administration. To confirm Mr. Bolton to the position of U.N. ambassador would fly in the face of the clear consensus of the country that a new direction is called for."[^cite_note-136]

### Termination of service

On December 4, 2006, Bolton announced that he would terminate his work as U.S. representative to the UN at the end of the recess appointment and would not continue to seek confirmation.[^cite_note-137] His letter of resignation from the Bush administration was accepted on December 4, 2006, effective when his recess appointment ended December 9 at the formal adjournment of the [109th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/109th_United_States_Congress).

The announcement was characterized as Bolton's "resignation" by the Associated Press,[^cite_note-138] United Press International,[^cite_note-139] ABC News,[^cite_note-140] and other news sources, as well as a White House press release[^cite_note-bushaccept-11] and President Bush himself.[^cite_note-bushmeets-141] The White House, however, later objected to the use of this language. Deputy Press Secretary [Dana Perino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dana_Perino) told [CBS News](359551.md) "it is not a resignation."[^cite_note-142] The actual language of the President's written acceptance was: "It is with deep regret that I accept John Bolton's decision to end his service in the administration as permanent representative of the United States to the United Nations when his commission expires." However, at a press conference, the President said, "I received the resignation of Ambassador John Bolton. I accept it. I'm not happy about it. I think he deserved to be confirmed."[^cite_note-bushmeets-141] Some news organizations subsequently altered their language to phrases such as "to step down," "to leave," or "to exit."[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145]



John Bolton in 2008

### Support for Bolton

Upon the reveal of Bolton as the nominee, [Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice_as_Secretary_of_State) explained that "\[t]he president and I have asked John to do this work because he knows how to get things done." Further, she explained that she saw Bolton "as a tough-minded diplomat" with "a strong record of success" bolstered by "a proven track record of effective multilateralism." [Nile Gardiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Gardiner), then a security specialist at the [Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation), explained that "John Bolton will be a U.S. ambassador who aggressively pursues the U.S. national interest at the United Nations." He viewed Bolton as someone who would "be tenacious and aggressive in pursuing the president's goals."[^cite_note-\:16-146]

During his confirmation hearings in 2005, letters with signatures of more than 64 co-workers and professional colleagues were sent to Senator [Richard Lugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Lugar), Chairman of the [Senate Foreign Relations Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Foreign_Relations_Committee), in praise of Bolton and contradicting other criticisms and allegations concerning his diplomatic style and his treatment of colleagues and staff.[^cite_note-147]

In late 2006, when his nomination was again before the committee, another letter signed by professional colleagues supporting the renomination was sent to Senator Lugar.[^cite_note-148]

In a *[New York Times](30680.md)* article from 2018 (shortly after when he was announced to be President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s pick for [National Security Advisor](44028.md)), multiple figures in Bolton's appointment fight came forward to share what they thought about him in 2005-2006. Dan Diller, deputy staff director for the Senate Foreign Relations Committee Republican majority, wrote to the *Times,* explaining that their investigation "found no clear ethical transgressions." In his view, Bolton "was an aggressive, sharp-elbowed bureaucratic operator who always tried to get his way, but would relent in the end rather than engaging in insubordination."[^cite_note-149]

## National Security Advisor (2018–2019)

### Speculation on position (2016–2017)

In an interview with conservative radio host [Hugh Hewitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Hewitt) during the [2016 U.S. presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), Republican nominee [Donald Trump](4848272.md) named Bolton as a possible choice for [Secretary of State](32293.md). Appearing on Fox News' Fox and Friends on December 1, 2016, Bolton admitted he was being considered as a Secretary of State candidate for the incoming Trump administration.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-nytdec4-151] Several Trump associates claim Bolton was not chosen, in part, due to Trump's disdain for Bolton's signature mustache.[^cite_note-152]



May 2, 2018: (from left) Bolton, [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo), President Trump, Vice President Pence

The evening of December 10, the [BBC](19344654.md) cited [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC) reports that "sources close to Mr Trump \[were] ... saying that Mr Tillerson is likely to be named next week" and that former [UN ambassador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Nations) John Bolton "will serve as his deputy".[^cite_note-153]

Bolton has supported theories about the health of Hillary Clinton and about her aide [Huma Abedin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huma_Abedin), and in December 2016, Bolton said the conclusion of the [United States Intelligence Community](501244.md) that Russian hackers had intervened to help elect Donald Trump in 2016 may have been a "[false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag)" operation.[^cite_note-154] In a subsequent interview on Fox News, Bolton criticized the Obama administration's retaliatory sanctions as insufficient and suggested that the US response should "make them \[the Russians] feel pain".[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]

### Tenure

In February 2017 President Trump interviewed Bolton and three others to determine who would fill the position of [National Security Advisor](44028.md) vacated by Lt. Gen. [Michael T. Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Flynn).[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] The position ultimately went to [H. R. McMaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._R._McMaster).[^cite_note-159] Trump "made a point on Monday of praising Mr. Bolton and saying that he would find a position for him in his administration eventually."[^cite_note-160] Bolton was seen at the White House on the evening of March 6, 2018, presumably to be interviewed as a candidate for national security adviser.[^cite_note-161]



U.S. Defense Secretary [James N. Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Mattis) greets the National Security Advisor-designate Bolton at [the Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) in March 2018.

*The New York Times* reported on March 22, 2018, that John Bolton would replace National Security Adviser H. R. McMaster, which was confirmed by Trump in a [tweet](9988187.md) later that day. Bolton began his position as National Security Advisor on April 9, 2018.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-\:0-163] *The New York Times* wrote that the rise of Bolton and [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo), coupled with the departure of Rex Tillerson and General McMaster, meant Trump's foreign policy team was now "the most radically aggressive foreign policy team around the American president in modern memory", and compared it to the foreign policy team surrounding [George W. Bush](3414021.md), notably with [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) and [Donald Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld).[^cite_note-164]

On April 10, 2018, [Homeland Security Advisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland_Security_Advisor) [Tom Bossert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Bossert) resigned at Bolton's request,[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] and Bolton said he was considering a merger of the NSC with the Homeland Security Council.[^cite_note-another-167] During his first week in office Bolton requested and obtained the resignations of multiple [National Security Council](44026.md) (NSC) employees including NSC spokesman [Michael Anton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Anton) (April 8), deputy national security adviser [Nadia Schadlow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadia_Schadlow) (April 10), and deputy national security adviser [Ricky L. Waddell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricky_L._Waddell) (April 12).[^cite_note-another-167] [CNN](62028.md) reported in September 2018 that Bolton had significantly shrunk the number of NSC personnel, cutting it to under 300.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]

In April 2018, Bolton pressed President Trump to withdraw from the [Iran Nuclear Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action), which Trump did a month later.[^cite_note-Iran2-170]

The [Huffington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HuffPost) reported that on May 8, 2018, Bolton removed [Timothy Ziemer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._Timothy_Ziemer) and dissolved his Global Health Security team formerly on the NSC leaving the administration's high level preparation for and ability to respond to [pandemics](24255.md), infectious disease, and other biological threats unclear.[^cite_note-\:12-171] The choice to remove and not replace Ziemer in the midst of an emerging [Ebola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease) outbreak in [The Democratic Republic of Congo](76762.md) was criticized in several news outlets.[^cite_note-\:12-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]



December 26, 2018: Bolton and President Trump on the phone with Iraq Prime Minister [Adil Abdul-Mahdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adil_Abdul-Mahdi) during visit to U.S. troops

On September 10, 2018, in his first major address as National Security Advisor, Bolton criticized the [International Criminal Court](14880.md), saying it lacks checks and balances, exercises "jurisdiction over crimes that have disputed and ambiguous definitions," and has failed to "deter and punish atrocity crimes." Calling the ICC a threat to "American sovereignty and U.S. national security," Bolton said it is "superfluous," given that "domestic judicial systems already hold American citizens to the highest legal and ethical standards." He added that the U.S. would do everything "to protect our citizens" should the ICC attempt to prosecute U.S. servicemen over alleged [detainee abuse in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagram_torture_and_prisoner_abuse), and it would bar ICC judges and prosecutors from entering the U.S. and [sanction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_\(law\)) their funds. He also criticized [Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_government) efforts to bring [Israel](9282173.md) before the ICC over allegations of [human rights](13831.md) abuses in the occupied [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza](12047.md).[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177]



Trump, Bolton and [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) at the [2018 NATO summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brussels_summit) in Brussels

In 2018, Bolton requested that the Pentagon provide the White House with options for military strikes against Iran. According to the *New York Times*, Bolton "intensified the administration's policy of isolating and pressuring Iran—reflecting an animus against Iran's leaders that dates back to his days as an official in the George W. Bush administration. As a private citizen, he later called for military strikes on Iran, as well as regime change."[^cite_note-178]

In November 2018, Bolton said that an audio recording related to the [assassination of Jamal Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Jamal_Khashoggi) did not prove that [Saudi Arabian](349303.md) Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md) ordered the murder.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180]

As National Security Advisor, Bolton eliminated the kinds of internal policy debates that his predecessor H. R. McMaster had in place. *The New York Times* writes that this change in practices contributed to Trump's sudden decision to withdraw the United States from Syria in January 2019.[^cite_note-181]

As National Security Advisor, Bolton advanced policies skeptical and hostile toward international organizations.[^cite_note-LivingTheDream-182] By his first year as National Security Advisor, Bolton had reshaped the National Security Council and become influential within the Trump Administration.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185]

By May 2019, Trump had undercut some of Bolton's major hard line positions, stating he was not seeking regime change in Iran and contradicting Bolton's assertion that North Korea had recently violated United Nations resolutions by testing new short-range missiles.[^cite_note-186]



In 2019, John Bolton, then U.S. National Security Advisor meeting United Kingdom Chancellor of the Exchequer [Sajid Javid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajid_Javid) (left) at [11 Downing Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11_Downing_Street)

In late August 2019, Bolton met [Alexander Lukashenko](156589.md) in [Minsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minsk), [Belarus](3457.md).[^cite_note-bolbel-187]

On September 10, 2019, President Trump claimed on Twitter that he had told Bolton on September 9 his "services are no longer needed" given "many" disagreements with Trump, thus Bolton gave his resignation on September 10. Just minutes later, Bolton contradicted Trump's account, tweeting out this claim: Bolton offered to resign on September 9, with Trump replying: "Let's talk about it tomorrow." Bolton later told the media Trump "never asked" for his resignation "directly or indirectly", and that he had both offered to resign and actually resigned of his own accord. Meanwhile, the White House endorsed Trump's version of the events.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] [Politico](9172097.md) reported that [Tucker Carlson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson) had urged Trump to fire Bolton.[^cite_note-190]

After Bolton's departure, Trump claimed that Bolton's views were "not necessarily tougher" than his own: "in some cases, he thought it was too tough what we were doing". On Cuba and Venezuela, Trump claimed that his own views were "far stronger" than Bolton's: "He was holding me back!" Bolton himself was known for his hawkish positions, including on Cuba and Venezuela, while Trump previously in May 2019 offered a different view of Bolton: "I actually temper John, which is pretty amazing."[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192]

Following his departure, Bolton was reappointed as a senior advisor to investment company [Rhône Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne_Group), a role he had held previously.[^cite_note-193]

Since leaving the first Trump Administration, Bolton has been harshly critical of Donald Trump, with a 2023 edition of his memoirs criticizing Trump as a man who punishes "personal enemies and appease\[s] adversaries Russia and China".[^cite_note-194]

Following Bolton's departure, [Fiona Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiona_Hill_\(presidential_advisor\)), who served as the National Security Council's senior director for Europe and Russia, testified before a Congressional committee that Bolton had disassociated himself from what he viewed as the Trump administration's effort to pressure Ukraine into investigating the President's political rivals.[^cite_note-195]

Bolton refused to attend his scheduled deposition in the [impeachment inquiry against Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_against_Donald_Trump) on November 7, 2019, and threatened to take legal action if he was subpoenaed.[^cite_note-196] Bolton said he was willing to testify, but wanted a federal court to first rule on a lawsuit by his former deputy seeking a court ruling on the competing claims of the Trump administration and Congress.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] However, in a written statement obtained by NBC News on January 6, 2020, Bolton announced that he would testify during the [Senate impeachment trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_trial_of_Donald_Trump) should he be issued a subpoena to do so.[^cite_note-nbcstatement-199][^cite_note-nytreportsstatement-200][^cite_note-cnnreportsstatement-201] A 51-vote majority would be required from the Senate in order to obtain the subpoena.[^cite_note-202]

As the second week of opening statements was set to begin, to be followed by a vote on whether to call witnesses, *The New York Times* reported that Bolton wrote in his forthcoming book that the President had told him in August 2019 that he wanted to continue freezing the Ukraine aid until officials there pursued investigations into Democrats, including the Bidens.[^cite_note-Timesbolton-13] The *Times* also stated that "Drafts of the book outline the potential testimony of the former national security adviser if he were called as a witness in the president's impeachment trial."[^cite_note-Timesbolton-13] On January 23, as Bolton was preparing for possible Senate testimony about the assertions in his manuscript, the National Security Council informed him that it contained classified information and "may not be published or otherwise disclosed without the deletion of this classified information.”[^cite_note-203] Bolton's attorney stated he did not believe the manuscript contained sensitive information.[^cite_note-204] The dispute set the stage for a prolonged review, with Bolton's attorney asking that access to the manuscript be limited to "those career government officials and employees regularly charged with responsibility for such reviews."[^cite_note-205] The leaked contents from the book also resulted in Trump impeachment lawyer [Jay Sekulow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Sekulow) dismissing Bolton's claims as "inadmissible" when he argued before the Senate in defense of Trump on January 28, 2020.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Democratic trial manager [Adam Schiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Schiff) later remarked that Sekulow's assertion validated the argument that Bolton should be called to testify.[^cite_note-208]
